Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. I’ll try to be sensible—like, “Okay, make a list,” or “just pray about it,” or go pull weeds in the garden because that usually settles me a little. I’ll tell myself, “Just do the next practical thing.”

It started with the job, but it’s gone way beyond just that. I mean, I still think about being fired all the time, but then it turns into, “Will anyone ever hire me again?” and “What if parents heard something and now my name is ruined?” Preschool communities can be so small, you know—people talk at pickup, at church bake sales, in the grocery store line.

And then it jumps to retirement, like whether I’ve set myself back so badly I’ll never catch up. Or I’ll think about some tiny mistake I made during story time—like choosing the wrong book or losing my place or not noticing a child getting restless—and suddenly my mind says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” Which sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes. So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.

Yes… I think so. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.

But mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.

So yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.

That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. I’ll try to be sensible—like, “Okay, make a list,” or “just pray about it,” or go pull weeds in the garden because that usually settles me a little. I’ll tell myself, “Just do the next practical thing.”\n\nBut then the worry doesn’t really stop. It just sort of… changes hands. One thing passes it to the next thing, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon. I’ll look up and realize I was supposed to do something simple, like water the tomatoes—though the tomatoes are another whole issue this year—and instead I’ve been standing there going over things in my head. So, yes, controlling it is very hard.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. That was about… a year and two months ago now, I guess.\n\nI mean, I’d had stressful school years before—every teacher has, especially with preschoolers, because one minute you’re doing finger paints and the next someone’s crying because their mitten is “wrong”—but this was different. After I lost that job, the worrying just kind of settled in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Have these hard-to-control worries been present most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, since I was fired from the preschool. I remember because it was right after that whole ending of the school year—well, not even a normal ending, really—and I kept thinking it would ease up after a few weeks, like when you get through a bad parent conference or a chaotic holiday program. But it just… didn’t. Most days it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worry is mostly about losing that preschool job and what came from it, or does it go well beyond that?\n\nPatient: It started with the job, but it’s gone way beyond just that. I mean, I still think about being fired all the time, but then it turns into, “Will anyone ever hire me again?” and “What if parents heard something and now my name is ruined?” Preschool communities can be so small, you know—people talk at pickup, at church bake sales, in the grocery store line.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether I’ve set myself back so badly I’ll never catch up. Or I’ll think about some tiny mistake I made during story time—like choosing the wrong book or losing my place or not noticing a child getting restless—and suddenly my mind says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” Which sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes. So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, that’s actually a big part of why I finally came in. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations where the director stands in the corner with a clipboard and you suddenly forget how to read The Very Hungry Caterpillar.\n\nIt just doesn’t let up. By evening my jaw aches too, and I’ll try to play guitar—because that used to help me unwind—but sometimes I’m so tight I can barely enjoy it. I’m sitting there with the guitar in my lap thinking, “Relax your shoulders,” and then two minutes later they’re up around my ears again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is stronger than most people would have in your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. By evening my jaw aches, and I’ll try to play guitar, because that’s supposed to be my peaceful thing, you know? But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Most days it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worry is mostly about losing that preschool job and what came from it, or does it go well beyond that?\n\nPatient: It started with the job, but it’s gone way beyond just that. Or I’ll think about some tiny mistake I made during story time—like choosing the wrong book or losing my place or not noticing a child getting restless—and suddenly my mind says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” Which sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes. So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. By evening my jaw aches too, and I’ll try to play guitar—because that used to help me unwind—but sometimes I’m so tight I can barely enjoy it. I’m sitting there with the guitar in my lap thinking, “Relax your shoulders,” and then two minutes later they’re up around my ears again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is stronger than most people would have in your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. By evening my jaw aches, and I’ll try to play guitar, because that’s supposed to be my peaceful thing, you know? But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you already a chronic worrier?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who thinks ahead too much, like planning a classroom craft and then realizing you’ve bought enough glue sticks for a small nation. But this level of worrying—where it feels constant and hard to shake—that really started after I was fired from the preschool.\n\nSo… about a year and two months now. That’s when it became this daily thing.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s gotten in the way of the exact things I’m supposed to be doing to fix the situation, which is… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I keep fussing over the wording or worrying I’m not ready to send it. I second-guess everything I say.\n\nEven volunteering for story-time, which should be simple for me, I don’t really do unless someone else handles the planning. So yes… it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady, reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired easily?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. Yes. That one, yes.

Like if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.

It happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll be trying to plan a story or activity and then suddenly I’m thinking about how it would be judged, whether I’ve forgotten a step, whether someone would look at it and think I don’t know what I’m doing. I used to be able to do that kind of thing almost automatically—well, not automatically, but you know, with the usual mess of crayons and paper and someone needing help with scissors—and now my attention just keeps darting away. So yes, concentrating has been hard because the worry keeps butting in.

So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, that’s actually a big part of why I finally came in. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations where the director stands in the corner with a clipboard and you suddenly forget how to read The Very Hungry Caterpillar.\n\nIt just doesn’t let up. By evening my jaw aches too, and I’ll try to play guitar—because that used to help me unwind—but sometimes I’m so tight I can barely enjoy it. I’m sitting there with the guitar in my lap thinking, “Relax your shoulders,” and then two minutes later they’re up around my ears again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is stronger than most people would have in your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. By evening my jaw aches, and I’ll try to play guitar, because that’s supposed to be my peaceful thing, you know? But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you already a chronic worrier?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who thinks ahead too much, like planning a classroom craft and then realizing you’ve bought enough glue sticks for a small nation. But this level of worrying—where it feels constant and hard to shake—that really started after I was fired from the preschool.\n\nSo… about a year and two months now. That’s when it became this daily thing.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s gotten in the way of the exact things I’m supposed to be doing to fix the situation, which is… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I keep fussing over the wording or worrying I’m not ready to send it. And interviews—oh, I used to talk to parents and kids all day without thinking twice, you know, explaining why someone’s child came home with blue paint in their hair—but now I stumble through them. I second-guess everything I say.\n\nEven volunteering for story-time, which should be simple for me, I don’t really do unless someone else handles the planning. So yes… it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady, reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired easily?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I do feel worn out a lot, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “tired easily,” exactly.\n\nSome days I get halfway through something simple and feel like I need to sit down, but then other times I can keep going if I’m distracted. It’s hard to separate, you know? Like when I used to run around a classroom all morning, that kind of tired made sense—glitter everywhere, snack time chaos, someone crying because the blocks “looked at him wrong.” This is different, maybe. Or maybe I’m overthinking the wording of the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is occupied by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one, yes.\n\nLike if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.\n\nIt happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll be trying to plan a story or activity and then suddenly I’m thinking about how it would be judged, whether I’ve forgotten a step, whether someone would look at it and think I don’t know what I’m doing. I used to be able to do that kind of thing almost automatically—well, not automatically, but you know, with the usual mess of crayons and paper and someone needing help with scissors—and now my attention just keeps darting away. So yes, concentrating has been hard because the worry keeps butting in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Most nights, honestly.

I get into bed and my body’s tired, but my head just… keeps going. I’ll replay interview answers, like, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Did I sound too uncertain?” And then it turns into bills, and those licensing forms—there’s always some form, some little box that feels like it could ruin everything if I fill it out wrong.

It reminds me of when I used to stay late cutting out shapes for bulletin boards, except then at least there was something to show for it, you know? Now I’m just lying there for hours, staring into the dark, doing mental arithmetic and imaginary conversations.

And even when I do fall asleep, I’m waking up around four in the morning already tense, already calculating what could go wrong that day. So… yes, sleep’s been pretty bad.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.9, F43.23, F45.8, F45.41\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, hi… I guess what finally pushed me to come in is this constant clenching. My shoulders and neck are tight all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends. I teach little kids, and you know, you’re always up and down, tying shoes, sitting on tiny chairs—those chairs really do a number on you—but this feels different.\n\nBy the evening my jaw is aching too, and that’s been bothering me because I like to play guitar when I get home, just to unwind, but lately it hurts so much I can hardly enjoy it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about different things, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I do. It’s like my mind doesn’t stay on one thing—it sort of pinballs around. I’ll start worrying about whether I’ll ever get hired again, and then it turns into, you know, did I ruin my reputation with parents, and then retirement—whether that’s slipping away somehow.\n\nAnd then it gets ridiculous, but it doesn’t feel ridiculous in the moment. Like I’ll think back to story time and wonder if I made some tiny mistake in an activity, and suddenly my brain is saying, “Well, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children.” Which… I know that sounds harsh. I used to have this one felt-board story the kids loved—sorry, that’s not really the point—but yes, it’s a lot of different things, even when nothing is actually happening right then.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s probably the worst part of it, honestly. I’ll try to be sensible—like, “Okay, make a list,” or “just pray about it,” or go pull weeds in the garden because that usually settles me a little. I’ll tell myself, “Just do the next practical thing.”\n\nBut then the worry doesn’t really stop. It just sort of… changes hands. One thing passes it to the next thing, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon. I’ll look up and realize I was supposed to do something simple, like water the tomatoes—though the tomatoes are another whole issue this year—and instead I’ve been standing there going over things in my head. So, yes, controlling it is very hard.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. That was about… a year and two months ago now, I guess.\n\nI mean, I’d had stressful school years before—every teacher has, especially with preschoolers, because one minute you’re doing finger paints and the next someone’s crying because their mitten is “wrong”—but this was different. After I lost that job, the worrying just kind of settled in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Have these hard-to-control worries been present most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, since I was fired from the preschool. I remember because it was right after that whole ending of the school year—well, not even a normal ending, really—and I kept thinking it would ease up after a few weeks, like when you get through a bad parent conference or a chaotic holiday program. But it just… didn’t. Most days it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worry is mostly about losing that preschool job and what came from it, or does it go well beyond that?\n\nPatient: It started with the job, but it’s gone way beyond just that. I mean, I still think about being fired all the time, but then it turns into, “Will anyone ever hire me again?” and “What if parents heard something and now my name is ruined?” Preschool communities can be so small, you know—people talk at pickup, at church bake sales, in the grocery store line.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether I’ve set myself back so badly I’ll never catch up. Or I’ll think about some tiny mistake I made during story time—like choosing the wrong book or losing my place or not noticing a child getting restless—and suddenly my mind says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” Which sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes. So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, that’s actually a big part of why I finally came in. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations where the director stands in the corner with a clipboard and you suddenly forget how to read The Very Hungry Caterpillar.\n\nIt just doesn’t let up. By evening my jaw aches too, and I’ll try to play guitar—because that used to help me unwind—but sometimes I’m so tight I can barely enjoy it. I’m sitting there with the guitar in my lap thinking, “Relax your shoulders,” and then two minutes later they’re up around my ears again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is stronger than most people would have in your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. By evening my jaw aches, and I’ll try to play guitar, because that’s supposed to be my peaceful thing, you know? But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you already a chronic worrier?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who thinks ahead too much, like planning a classroom craft and then realizing you’ve bought enough glue sticks for a small nation. But this level of worrying—where it feels constant and hard to shake—that really started after I was fired from the preschool.\n\nSo… about a year and two months now. That’s when it became this daily thing.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s gotten in the way of the exact things I’m supposed to be doing to fix the situation, which is… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I keep fussing over the wording or worrying I’m not ready to send it. And interviews—oh, I used to talk to parents and kids all day without thinking twice, you know, explaining why someone’s child came home with blue paint in their hair—but now I stumble through them. I second-guess everything I say.\n\nEven volunteering for story-time, which should be simple for me, I don’t really do unless someone else handles the planning. So yes… it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady, reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired easily?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I do feel worn out a lot, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “tired easily,” exactly.\n\nSome days I get halfway through something simple and feel like I need to sit down, but then other times I can keep going if I’m distracted. It’s hard to separate, you know? Like when I used to run around a classroom all morning, that kind of tired made sense—glitter everywhere, snack time chaos, someone crying because the blocks “looked at him wrong.” This is different, maybe. Or maybe I’m overthinking the wording of the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is occupied by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one, yes.\n\nLike if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.\n\nIt happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll be trying to plan a story or activity and then suddenly I’m thinking about how it would be judged, whether I’ve forgotten a step, whether someone would look at it and think I don’t know what I’m doing. I used to be able to do that kind of thing almost automatically—well, not automatically, but you know, with the usual mess of crayons and paper and someone needing help with scissors—and now my attention just keeps darting away. So yes, concentrating has been hard because the worry keeps butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI get into bed and my body’s tired, but my head just… keeps going. I’ll replay interview answers, like, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Did I sound too uncertain?” And then it turns into bills, and those licensing forms—there’s always some form, some little box that feels like it could ruin everything if I fill it out wrong.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to stay late cutting out shapes for bulletin boards, except then at least there was something to show for it, you know? Now I’m just lying there for hours, staring into the dark, doing mental arithmetic and imaginary conversations.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I’m waking up around four in the morning already tense, already calculating what could go wrong that day. So… yes, sleep’s been pretty bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Or maybe I’m overthinking the wording of the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is occupied by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one, yes.\n\nLike if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.\n\nIt happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll be trying to plan a story or activity and then suddenly I’m thinking about how it would be judged, whether I’ve forgotten a step, whether someone would look at it and think I don’t know what I’m doing. I used to be able to do that kind of thing almost automatically—well, not automatically, but you know, with the usual mess of crayons and paper and someone needing help with scissors—and now my attention just keeps darting away. So yes, concentrating has been hard because the worry keeps butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI get into bed and my body’s tired, but my head just… keeps going. I’ll replay interview answers, like, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Did I sound too uncertain?” And then it turns into bills, and those licensing forms—there’s always some form, some little box that feels like it could ruin everything if I fill it out wrong.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to stay late cutting out shapes for bulletin boards, except then at least there was something to show for it, you know? Now I’m just lying there for hours, staring into the dark, doing mental arithmetic and imaginary conversations.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I’m waking up around four in the morning already tense, already calculating what could go wrong that day. So… yes, sleep’s been pretty bad.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been more irritable than usual?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / preschool_or_kindergarten_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, but then, who does, right?

There are moments where I catch myself sounding sharper than I meant to, like over something small, and then I feel bad about it. But I don’t know if that’s “more irritable than usual” or just… you know, being pressed. When I was teaching, you had to have this almost impossible level of patience—shoelaces, spilled juice, five children all saying your name at once—so I’m used to measuring myself against that version of me.

Lately I guess I don’t have as much room inside before I feel annoyed. But I’m not sure how much it shows.

I think… yes. I mean, if someone called tomorrow and said, “Here’s your position back, everything’s fine,” I’d probably be relieved for about ten minutes, and then my mind would just find the next thing.

I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you already a chronic worrier?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who thinks ahead too much, like planning a classroom craft and then realizing you’ve bought enough glue sticks for a small nation. But this level of worrying—where it feels constant and hard to shake—that really started after I was fired from the preschool.\n\nSo… about a year and two months now. And interviews—oh, I used to talk to parents and kids all day without thinking twice, you know, explaining why someone’s child came home with blue paint in their hair—but now I stumble through them. I second-guess everything I say.\n\nEven volunteering for story-time, which should be simple for me, I don’t really do unless someone else handles the planning. So yes… it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady, reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired easily?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate, you know? That one, yes.\n\nLike if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.\n\nIt happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll tell myself, “Just do the next practical thing.”\n\nBut then the worry doesn’t really stop. It just sort of… changes hands. One thing passes it to the next thing, and before I know it I’ve lost half the afternoon. I’ll look up and realize I was supposed to do something simple, like water the tomatoes—though the tomatoes are another whole issue this year—and instead I’ve been standing there going over things in my head. So, yes, controlling it is very hard.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries begin after a particular stressful event or change in your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It really started after I was fired from the preschool. That was about… a year and two months ago now, I guess.\n\nI mean, I’d had stressful school years before—every teacher has, especially with preschoolers, because one minute you’re doing finger paints and the next someone’s crying because their mitten is “wrong”—but this was different. After I lost that job, the worrying just kind of settled in and never really left.\n\nDoctor: Have these hard-to-control worries been present most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes… definitely more than six months. It’s been about a year and two months now, since I was fired from the preschool. I remember because it was right after that whole ending of the school year—well, not even a normal ending, really—and I kept thinking it would ease up after a few weeks, like when you get through a bad parent conference or a chaotic holiday program. But it just… didn’t. Most days it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worry is mostly about losing that preschool job and what came from it, or does it go well beyond that?\n\nPatient: It started with the job, but it’s gone way beyond just that. I mean, I still think about being fired all the time, but then it turns into, “Will anyone ever hire me again?” and “What if parents heard something and now my name is ruined?” Preschool communities can be so small, you know—people talk at pickup, at church bake sales, in the grocery store line.\n\nAnd then it jumps to retirement, like whether I’ve set myself back so badly I’ll never catch up. Or I’ll think about some tiny mistake I made during story time—like choosing the wrong book or losing my place or not noticing a child getting restless—and suddenly my mind says, “See, maybe you shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.” Which sounds harsh when I say it out loud, but that’s where my head goes. So no, it’s not just losing the job anymore. It spreads into everything connected to who I thought I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or unable to relax?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, that’s actually a big part of why I finally came in. My shoulders and neck are clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations where the director stands in the corner with a clipboard and you suddenly forget how to read The Very Hungry Caterpillar.\n\nIt just doesn’t let up. By evening my jaw aches too, and I’ll try to play guitar—because that used to help me unwind—but sometimes I’m so tight I can barely enjoy it. I’m sitting there with the guitar in my lap thinking, “Relax your shoulders,” and then two minutes later they’re up around my ears again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is stronger than most people would have in your situation?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I know anyone would worry after losing a job, especially in teaching, where your reputation sort of follows you around like glitter after an art project—you think you cleaned it up, and then there it is on your sleeve three days later.\n\nBut mine feels… bigger than what makes sense. My mind keeps going in circles: whether I’ll ever get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve messed up my retirement. And then it gets ridiculous but feels real in the moment—like I’ll remember a tiny mistake during story time, losing my place or picking an activity that didn’t go smoothly, and suddenly I’m thinking, maybe that means I shouldn’t be trusted with children at all.\n\nSo yes, I do think it’s stronger than what most people would have. It doesn’t stay in proportion.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying specifically about the neck, shoulder, or jaw tension itself?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do, but it’s not just the tension itself, if that makes sense. I notice it constantly—my shoulders and neck stay clenched all day, like I’m bracing for one of those classroom observations that never actually ends.\n\nAnd then I’ll start thinking about why I can’t just relax, and whether people can see it on me. By evening my jaw aches, and I’ll try to play guitar, because that’s supposed to be my peaceful thing, you know? But sometimes I can hardly enjoy it because I’m sitting there thinking about how tight everything feels instead of just playing.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost the preschool job, were you already a chronic worrier?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’ve always been the kind of person who thinks ahead too much, like planning a classroom craft and then realizing you’ve bought enough glue sticks for a small nation. But this level of worrying—where it feels constant and hard to shake—that really started after I was fired from the preschool.\n\nSo… about a year and two months now. That’s when it became this daily thing.\n\nDoctor: Has this worrying been interfering with your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s gotten in the way of the exact things I’m supposed to be doing to fix the situation, which is… embarrassing, honestly.\n\nI’ve missed application deadlines because I keep fussing over the wording or worrying I’m not ready to send it. And interviews—oh, I used to talk to parents and kids all day without thinking twice, you know, explaining why someone’s child came home with blue paint in their hair—but now I stumble through them. I second-guess everything I say.\n\nEven volunteering for story-time, which should be simple for me, I don’t really do unless someone else handles the planning. So yes… it’s keeping me from getting back into a classroom in any steady, reliable way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired easily?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I mean, I do feel worn out a lot, but I’m not sure if I’d call it “tired easily,” exactly.\n\nSome days I get halfway through something simple and feel like I need to sit down, but then other times I can keep going if I’m distracted. It’s hard to separate, you know? Like when I used to run around a classroom all morning, that kind of tired made sense—glitter everywhere, snack time chaos, someone crying because the blocks “looked at him wrong.” This is different, maybe. Or maybe I’m overthinking the wording of the question.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because your mind is occupied by worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one, yes.\n\nLike if I’m filling out an application, I’ll get stuck on one paragraph and read it over and over, and somehow I’m not taking in the words at all because my mind has already run off to, you know, “What if the administrator thinks this sounds desperate?” or “What if I left out some crucial detail?” And then I start tweaking one sentence for forty minutes.\n\nIt happens with lesson planning too, even something simple. I’ll be trying to plan a story or activity and then suddenly I’m thinking about how it would be judged, whether I’ve forgotten a step, whether someone would look at it and think I don’t know what I’m doing. I used to be able to do that kind of thing almost automatically—well, not automatically, but you know, with the usual mess of crayons and paper and someone needing help with scissors—and now my attention just keeps darting away. So yes, concentrating has been hard because the worry keeps butting in.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly.\n\nI get into bed and my body’s tired, but my head just… keeps going. I’ll replay interview answers, like, “Why did I say it that way?” or “Did I sound too uncertain?” And then it turns into bills, and those licensing forms—there’s always some form, some little box that feels like it could ruin everything if I fill it out wrong.\n\nIt reminds me of when I used to stay late cutting out shapes for bulletin boards, except then at least there was something to show for it, you know? Now I’m just lying there for hours, staring into the dark, doing mental arithmetic and imaginary conversations.\n\nAnd even when I do fall asleep, I’m waking up around four in the morning already tense, already calculating what could go wrong that day. So… yes, sleep’s been pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Have you been more irritable than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I don’t think of myself as an irritable person, but then, who does, right?\n\nThere are moments where I catch myself sounding sharper than I meant to, like over something small, and then I feel bad about it. But I don’t know if that’s “more irritable than usual” or just… you know, being pressed. When I was teaching, you had to have this almost impossible level of patience—shoelaces, spilled juice, five children all saying your name at once—so I’m used to measuring myself against that version of me.\n\nLately I guess I don’t have as much room inside before I feel annoyed. But I’m not sure how much it shows.\n\nDoctor: If the job loss were fully resolved tomorrow, do you think this worrying would still keep going?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, if someone called tomorrow and said, “Here’s your position back, everything’s fine,” I’d probably be relieved for about ten minutes, and then my mind would just find the next thing.\n\nIt keeps circling, not just on the job itself, but whether I’ll ever really get hired again, whether parents think badly of me now, whether I’ve damaged my reputation in some way I can’t fix. And then it jumps to retirement, like, “Have I ruined that too?” which is ridiculous maybe, but it feels very real at three in the afternoon when I’m supposed to be doing one simple errand.\n\nAnd it gets… smaller and stranger too. I’ll think back to a story-time activity, something tiny, like maybe I chose the wrong book or didn’t handle a child’s question well, and suddenly I’m thinking, “Does that mean I shouldn’t be trusted with children?” So yes, I don’t think resolving the job loss would switch it off. It would just move around."
